# Jesse Storm mentorship program

## You

I have a mentorship meeting coming up in one hour with jesse storm: `20_Progress/Mentorship Program/Jesse`, `20_Progress/Mentorship Program`, `20_Progress/Mentorship Program/Plans/Plan - Jesse`, `20_Progress/Mentorship Program/Plans/Overall Plan`, `20_Progress/Mentorship Program/Meetings`. First, the CPT program has been closed by the University of Minnesota, so there is no longer any opportunity for a junior‑year internship unless the rule changes on November 6, which is still undecided. We are waiting for the university’s decision on that date.

Notes on this matter are also in Jarvis if you’d like to review them. Our main agenda is to work through the current plan, taking into account the blocker just mentioned. The blocker will be discussed in the meeting in one hour.

We need to design the blocker, introduction, and elevator pitch for the meeting. The agenda includes:

- How exactly I will participate in the meeting  
- How I will interact with everyone and with the mentor  
- How I will introduce myself: the why, what, and how, giving a brief intro about myself  
- Expecting a one‑year mentorship program and determining what we will do  

The mentor‑program and internship agenda is not yet available, so we have many topics to discuss, such as job opportunities.

Based on the plan we established previously, I want to update Jessie’s folder. We need to learn from recent developments and draft:

- The perfect meeting  
- The perfect action template  
- The perfect brief  
- The transcript of the meeting  

All necessary information is provided, allowing us to use the manager program and its benefits during the meeting.

We should follow the standard workflows for this folder, updating the meeting, action, brief, and transcript standards according to the detailed schedule. Then, following Jarvis, we must ensure every item is properly documented.

## Claude

I'll start by mapping the mentorship folder and the 30_Order folder in parallel. This is read-only.

I changed nothing, so the base layer is not corrected yet. Everything below comes from your vault, your calendar and one Gmail search.

## Time-critical findings

- **The meeting is on your calendar.** "Mentorship Program Kickoff" runs today 4:30 to 5:15 pm CT on Zoom, with the Google Doc link in the invite. Jesse is the only guest and his RSVP still reads "needs action". You said "everyone", so tell me if others will be on the call.
- **I can't confirm he replied.** The connected Gmail (gupta21anant) has nothing from or to him in 14 days. Your outreach likely went from your UMN address, which I can't see.
- **His surname.** The vault says Jesse **Strom** (`Mentor Details'26`), not Storm. Check it before you say it aloud.
- **Major.** The same note lists your major as Computer Engineering (from the program form). Your email signature says Computer Science with an Entrepreneurship minor. Pick one for the intro.
- **Nov 6 is not a university decision.** Your vault (`CPT Program`, `CPT Broadcast Messages`) says it is a federal court hearing on the AAU lawsuit against DHS. The government's response is due today. UMN's ISSS is pausing CPT because of the August SEVP guidance, so the university isn't deciding anything on Nov 6.
- **"No internship opportunity" overstates your own research.** `Junior Year Internship Decision` (Oct 23, after the ISSS meeting) lists live routes:
  - on-campus research (core lane);
  - pre-completion OPT (decide around March 2027);
  - an internship physically abroad;
  - CPT returning later.
- **Mentoring Agreement.** Your SMART Goal 1 ("land a 2027-cycle internship") goes to Katie by Nov 2, four days before the hearing. Word it so it holds whichever way Nov 6 goes.
- **No links to the CPT notes.** `Plan - Jesse` and `Smart Goals` mention "the CPT/OPT bind" but link none of the 20+ notes in `10_Areas/Career/VISAs & Work Auth/`.

## What is actually in 30_Order for meetings

30_Order has 213 notes. Counting the 76 tooling files under `System/`, it has 280 files. The meeting kit is ten small notes:

| Kind | Notes | Location |
|---|---|---|
| Standards | Mentorship Meeting Standard (pre-meeting plan), Brief Standard, Action Standard | Internship/, Ingestion/, Ingestion/ |
| Templates | Mentorship Meeting Template, Brief Template, Action Template, For Transcript | Career/, Capability/, Capability/, Frontmatter/ |
| Workflows | Transcript to Brief, Brief to Action, Brief to Progress (project, not meeting) | Ingestion/ |

That is about 400 lines, roughly 4% of the notes. It feels bloated because it is scattered, incomplete and contradictory, not because it is big. The real weight is elsewhere:

- Courses: 49 notes.
- Internship: 35 notes.
- Classes templates: 15 notes.
- A stray `Application Document Preparation 1.md`.

Specific problems:
1. **There is no Transcript Standard.** "Transcript" is only a frontmatter starter. The registry treats it as a clipping subtype.
2. **The registry lists no workflow for the mentorship-meeting plan.** Brief and Action each have their own workflow, so the chain is two workflows plus one paragraph inside a Standard.
3. **The Playbook, the Standard's "After the Meeting", `Systems for Goals` Layer 2 and the Plan's Decisions line overlap.** Each meeting writes to about nine places. The Playbook has no Standard at all.
4. **The Mentorship Meeting Standard is Ahnaf-specific.** It hard-codes "alternate Mondays" and a gold example marked superseded. Its `legacy_match` points at a note that no longer exists.
5. **The skills contradict the Standards.** `/transcript-to-brief` and `/note-to-actions` cite paths without the `Ingestion/` and `Capability/` subfolders. They also tell you to set `input_kind: transcript` on the brief and to write `source_note` as a bare filename. The Standard says `input_kind: brief` and a quoted wikilink.
6. **The old notes are non-conformant:**
   - the 07-13 brief still carries `input_kind: transcript` and a plain-text `source_note`;
   - the 07-14 action note is typed `project`, with numbered headings and a "Standard doesn't exist yet" disclaimer;
   - both Playbooks are `type: project` with no `project_kind`.
7. **Other meetings follow other shapes.** ISSS (prep sheet, transcript, brief, no action note), CausalOps `Meetings/`, and the career-fair transcripts don't use the mentorship kit.

## Proposed design: 3 notes, 3 Standards, 1 Workflow

Per meeting, three notes: Transcript (raw, untouched), Brief, Actions. Per mentor, one thin index. I'd put each meeting in its own folder (`Jesse/Meetings/2026-10-28/`) with date-prefixed note names, since wikilinks need unique names.

| Keep or create | Remove or merge |
|---|---|
| **Transcript Standard** (new) and Transcript Template (from `For Transcript`) | Mentorship Meeting Standard and Template |
| Brief Standard and Template, revised | Transcript to Brief (workflow) |
| Action Standard and Template, kept general (it also governs `Gaps` and the W44 actions note) | Brief to Action (workflow) |
| **Meeting Workflow** (new): Before, During, After | |

- **Where the prep goes.** Fold the pre-meeting content (goal, intro, questions, ask) into the Brief as a "Before the Meeting" block, created before the call and completed after it. That drops the fourth note type. It changes what the Brief Standard says a brief is, so it's your call.
- **Removal footprint.** Mentorship Meeting Standard and Template are linked only from each other and the registry. Transcript to Brief and Brief to Action are linked from the registry, the workflows index, the Brief and Action Standards, and historical logs. Brief to Progress and the Clipping Standard stay.
- **Net saving.** The kit goes from 9 notes to 7, so the gain is coherence, not size.
- **Also needed:**
  - registry and lint-spec updates;
  - fixed skill paths and frontmatter rules;
  - the Playbook reduced to an index or retired;
  - the empty `Overall Plan` filled or deleted.

## Inputs for the meeting (all in the vault)

- **Agenda.** The Google Doc's starter agenda:
  - introductions;
  - Jesse's engagement style;
  - "what would you do in my position";
  - the 80/20 goals;
  - a cadence of every other week, 30 to 45 minutes;
  - fill out the Mentoring Agreement together.
- **Rules from the Ahnaf lessons.** Book the next meeting before the call ends, record what was said the same day, and send a thank-you within 24 hours.
- **Blocker framing.** Don't ask Jesse to fix CPT. Present three lanes (on-campus research, applying with authorization in writing, portfolio and contacts) and ask what he'd weight, plus whether SymphonyAI hosts international interns. The dated gates are in `Junior Year Internship Decision`.
- **Blocker risks.** Don't call a project a job. Don't accept an offer before the authorization route exists. ISSS warned against both.

## Decisions I need from you

1. Fold the prep into the Brief (my recommendation), or keep a separate prep note?
2. One folder per meeting (recommended), or keep the shared Transcripts, Briefs and Action folders?
3. Fill `Overall Plan` as the program-level plan, or delete it since the Board covers that?
4. Keep the Playbooks as a thin index, or retire them?

Once you decide, I can do the edits in this order: the kit in 30_Order first, then repair the Jesse and Ahnaf notes, then draft the meeting's Brief and intro. The new Brief would use the revised template, with the blocker, intro and pitch in its "Before" block.

````
You are a specialized assistant for Anant's Mentorship Program work.

**Mentorship Overview**:
- **Plan**: Mentorship Program Plan
- **Mentor Details**: Current mentor information
- **Elevator Pitch**: Personal pitch for mentorship
- **Mentorship Board**: Dashboard for mentorship progress

**Location**: `20_Progress/Mentorship Program/`

**When to Use This Skill**:
1. User asks about mentorship program
2. User needs help with mentorship prep
3. User wants to review mentorship goals
4. User needs help with elevator pitch

**Common Queries**:
- "What's in my mentorship program?"
- "Help me prepare for mentorship"
- "Review my mentorship goals"
- "How do I structure my elevator pitch?"

- [Goal 2]
````

## You

Let's lay it out correctly so that it is easy to read mid conversation and not get lost in the note.

## Claude

I'll read the writing standard first, then rewrite the note.

The file changed since I last read it. I'm reading it again before I overwrite it.

I rewrote `20_Progress/Mentorship Program/Jesse/Google Doc - First Meeting` as a call script. I haven't run `Vault Lint` on it, and I didn't open the note again after saving.

I kept the filename because four notes link to it. The title is now "First Meeting Script, Jesse".

The note is built to scan mid-call. It opens with a "If I lose my place" box: leave with the next meeting booked, the Mentoring Agreement settled, and one real answer on the internship problem. A time-boxed running order follows, with a cut order if you run short. Spoken lines are in quotes and everything else is a cue.

**Sections, in call order:**
- **Open (2 min):** a short thank-you, plus asking permission before you record or transcribe.
- **Who I am (5 min):** a 20-second pitch and a 60-second pitch split into why, what and how.
- **About Jesse (8 min):** six conversation starters from his program profile. Each one is something he wrote or did, phrased as a question to ask, not a fact to recite.
- **The three goals (10 min):** the three goals as a plain-language walk-through, the two questions from the doc, and the five smaller goals as optional drafts.
- **Internship and CPT (10 min):** a four-sentence statement of the constraint, four questions to pick two from, one ask, and a "Do not" list.
- **Agreement and cadence (6 min):** the Nov 2 deadline and the cadence.
- **Close (3 min).**
- **After the call:** a four-line capture block and an after-the-meeting checklist.

**Fixes and changes:**
- **The CPT framing is corrected.** It says Nov 6 is a federal court hearing, not a university decision. It lists three live routes (on-campus research, an internship abroad, pre-completion OPT decided around March), not "no opportunity."
- **Agreement Goal 1.** The wording I proposed holds whichever way Nov 6 goes: secure a 2027 internship offer with a written work authorization route, and build the profile for a senior-year search if the summer route stays closed.
- **The old mirror is gone.** I replaced the Google Doc mirror and the stale "Suggestions" with a short record of what the doc told Jesse. The doc's two known gaps are still noted: the empty scheduling heading and the blank "20" item.
- **Frontmatter.** I kept the existing keys. I updated `updated` and `next`, added a `meeting-prep` tag, and added links to the Playbook, `Junior Year Internship Decision`, `CPT Program` and `ISSS October Meeting Brief` in `related_progress`.

**Confirm before the call.** The note marks two spots in the intro in brackets to check:
- Your major. The signature says Computer Science with an Entrepreneurship minor, but the mentor-program form says Computer Engineering.
- How to word your research role. It comes from the July Playbook.

The project one-liners also come from that Playbook, so skim them for anything that moved. I left out test counts and user numbers on purpose.

**Left alone:**

- **Note type.** I did not tag it `project_kind: plan`, because the Mentorship Meeting Standard's headings don't fit a script. That note-type question depends on the meeting-kit decision from my last message.
- **Session log.** I skipped it, per your rule against writing anything you didn't ask for.

```
# First Meeting Script, Jesse
Read this top to bottom during the call. Spoken lines are in quotes. Everything else is a cue. The shared Google Doc is still the agenda Jesse has; this note is only for me.
## If I lose my place
Leave the call with three things. Everything else can wait for meeting two.
1. **Next meeting booked**, with a day and time, before we hang up.
2. **Mentoring Agreement settled**: three goals agreed and how it gets signed and sent to Katie by **Nov 2**.
3. **One real answer on the internship problem**, from Jesse's own experience.
> [!tip] Running order and time (45 min block)
> 0:00 Open → 0:03 Intro → 0:08 About Jesse → 0:16 The three goals → 0:26 Internship and CPT → 0:36 Agreement and cadence → 0:42 Close
> **If short on time, cut in this order:** the five smaller goals, Nov 4 event, rapport extras. **Never cut:** Agreement, CPT, booking.
## 0:00 Open (2 min)
> "Thanks for making time, Jesse. I know you volunteered for this, and I want to use the time well. Have you had a chance to look at the doc I sent? I'll walk through it, but stop me anywhere."

Then ask once: **"Is it okay if I take notes as we talk?"** Record or transcribe only if he says yes.
## 0:03 Who I am (5 min)
### 20 second version
> "I'm Anant, a junior at UMN [confirm: Computer Science with an Entrepreneurship minor]. I build AI systems, mostly on my own. I want an internship at a company making serious AI products, and I'm here because I can build but I'm weaker on the professional side: pitching, networking, and knowing what hiring managers actually look for."
### 60 second version
**Why**
> "I want to land an internship at a large AI company and build a professional presence that doesn't exist yet. My starting point is honest: I can ship, but I have no network, no real LinkedIn, and almost no outside feedback on my work."

**What**
> "Right now I work on three things. Jarvis is an AI knowledge system I run on my own notes, with agents that search and maintain them. CausalOps is a causal-reasoning engine for security incidents that refuses to answer when the evidence is weak. TradingView is a personal stock research tool with an evidence-based scoring layer. I also do applied ML research at UMN [confirm role wording before saying it] and I keep a portfolio site at anantgupta.dev."

**How**
> "I work alone with AI coding agents, write everything down, and treat goals as systems with check-ins. That's why I suggested every other week and a short agenda each time."
### One extra line, only if it fits
> "In two hackathons the hardest part wasn't the code. It was explaining what was real and what was mocked. That's why Marketing is one of my three goals."

> [!warning] Before I say any of this
> The project facts above come from the July Playbook. Skim each project before the call and change anything that moved. Do not state test counts or user numbers from memory.
>
> Do not criticize last year's mentor. If asked: "I learned goals fade without a tracking system, so I came in with one."
## 0:08 About Jesse (8 min)
Source: his program profile in [[Mentor Details'26]]. Ask about it, never recite it. Pick three.
1. **His own invitation.** He wrote he wants a mentee "interested in the intersection of technology and manufacturing with AI."
   > "You said you wanted someone interested in manufacturing and AI. I'm closer to the AI and software side than manufacturing, so what were you hoping a mentee would bring?"
2. **Same start as me.** He began as a Systems Engineer out of UMN, 19 years ago.
   > "What did you not know at my stage that you wish someone had told you?"
3. **Where AI works on a plant floor.** He sells an AI-powered Data Operations and Manufacturing Operations Management (MOM) platform.
   > "Where is AI actually working in manufacturing today, and where is it still a demo?"
4. **Demos and travel.** He travels to customer sites, runs pre-sales demos, and describes himself as involved "from cradle to grave."
   > "When you demo to a customer who's skeptical, what makes it land? That's the skill I most want to learn."
5. **Change management.** His earlier specialty was technical leadership and change management.
   > "How do you get a team to adopt something they didn't ask for?"
6. **UMN.** He was an admissions ambassador.
   > "What made you want to stay involved with UMN students?"

> [!warning] Do not lead with CausalOps as a manufacturing story
> [[Initial Outreach]] flagged that link as unproven. If he asks, say it is about evidence before a decision and ask him whether that idea matters on a factory floor.
## 0:16 The three goals (10 min)
> "I think of goals as systems with a check-in, not intentions. Here's my starting draft. Tell me where you'd point me differently."

**1. Internships.** A 2027 internship at a larger AI-forward company: interview prep, LinkedIn and networking, a real review of my resume and portfolio.
**2. Project and startup judgment.** Outside feedback on Jarvis, CausalOps and TradingView, plus how he scopes and delivers a project start to finish.
**3. Marketing and communication.** Pitching clearly and running a good meeting. The shortcuts people who do this well actually use.

Then ask the question from the doc, word for word:
> "Does this match what you'd want to spend your time on, or would you point it somewhere else?"
### The five smaller goals (skip if short on time)
> "Under those three I want five small goals, and I left that section open on purpose so we can pick them together."

Drafts if he wants options, from [[Smart Goals]]:
- LinkedIn and posting rhythm (500 connections, one post a week).
- A monthly mock interview, alternating coding and system design.
- One startup idea tested start to finish with him.
- One event or meetup per quarter.
- Fifth slot left open.
## 0:26 Internship and the CPT problem (10 min)
**Frame:** I am not asking him to fix this. I want his judgment and, if he has one, an introduction.
### Say it in four sentences
> "Here's the constraint. I'm an international student, and UMN paused CPT in August after new federal guidance. CPT was how most students did a summer internship. There's a federal court hearing on November 6, and nobody, including our international office, knows what happens."

> "So I'm planning for three routes at once: research work on campus, an internship that physically happens abroad, and pre-completion OPT, which I'd decide on around March. I'm still applying everywhere in the meantime."

Do not say more than this unless he asks. Facts and dates: [[Junior Year Internship Decision]], [[CPT Program]], [[ISSS October Meeting Brief]].
### Ask him (pick two)
1. "Have you seen international interns or new grads handled well at your company or others? What did the company actually do?"
2. "If my summer ends up as research or a serious project instead of an internship, how do I make that read as strong to a hiring manager?"
3. "Would a later start date, like July, be realistic for a company like yours?"
4. "How would you weigh a smaller company that can work around this against a big name that can't?"
### The one ask
> "Is there anyone in your network who has hired international students that I could talk to for fifteen minutes?"

He is not expected to provide jobs. The ask is advice or an introduction.
### Do not
- Say CPT is "closed forever" or that the court will fix it. Nov 6 is a hearing, not a decision, and not the university's.
- Call a personal or volunteer project a job. ISSS warned this can hurt OPT eligibility.
- Accept or imply any start date before the work authorization exists in writing.
## 0:36 Mentoring Agreement and cadence (6 min)
**Agreement.** Three SMART goals, each with how the mentor helps. Due to Katie (ksobo@umn.edu) by **Nov 2**.
> "The form needs three goals by Monday. I'd like to fill it out with you now. How do you prefer to sign and send it?"

**Goal 1 wording to propose.** It has to hold whichever way Nov 6 goes:
> "Secure a 2027 internship offer with a written work authorization route, and build the profile for a senior-year search if the summer route stays closed."

Goals 2 and 3 are in [[Smart Goals]] under "Mapping to the CSE Mentoring Agreement."

**Cadence.** Every other week, 30 to 45 minutes.
> "Can we pick the weekday and time now so I can send the invite before we hang up? And would you be open to meeting in person around campus next time?"
## 0:42 Close (3 min)
1. **Book it.** Next date and time said out loud and put on the calendar.
2. **Optional:** the Meet Your Partner Event, Wed Nov 4, 7 to 8:30 pm, McNamara Alumni Center. "Are you planning to go? No pressure."
3. **State my next step:**
   > "My next step is to send you the signed agreement, and the notes from today, by [day]. Thank you. This is exactly what I hoped for."
```

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - The vault is an Obsidian second brain ("Jarvis"). Every note follows the `30_Order` kit: Template (scaffold), Standard (with a JSON Spec block read by Vault Lint), and Workflow. `Note Type Registry.md` maps each family to its Template, Standard, Workflow and folder.
   - Frontmatter vocabulary comes only from `Frontmatter Standard` (`type`, `status`, `*_kind`). Notes with `status: tree` need proposals before changes. Wikilinks resolve by filename, so names must be unique. Edits patch by heading. No extra blank lines (formatting rule: extra blank lines are errors).
   - Meeting loop in the registry:
     - `Mentorship Meeting Standard`/`Template` = pre-meeting plan (`type: project`, `project_kind: plan`), headings Meeting Focus / Demo and State / Questions / Ask / After the Meeting. The registry lists no workflow for it.
     - Transcript = frontmatter-only starter `For Transcript` (`type: input`, `input_kind: transcript`). It has no Standard of its own and is treated as a clipping subtype.
     - Brief = `Brief Standard`/`Brief Template`, workflow `Transcript to Brief` (`type: input`, `input_kind: brief`, `source_note` as a quoted wikilink).
     - Action = `Action Standard`/`Action Template`, workflow `Brief to Action` (`type: action`; also used outside meetings, e.g. `Gaps`, the W44 actions note).
   - Skills: `/transcript-to-brief` and `/note-to-actions`. Both cite wrong paths (missing the `Ingestion/` and `Capability/` subfolders) and contradict the Standards. They tell you to set `input_kind: transcript` on the brief and to write `source_note` as a bare filename with extension. The Standards say `input_kind: brief` and a quoted wikilink. The `mentorship` skill is a 1 KB stub pointing at notes that don't exist.
   - Writing rules:
     - `HUMAN_WRITING.md`: concrete over polished, no filler, honest uncertainty, avoid words like leverage, robust, journey, unlock, powerful.
     - Global user rule: NEVER use em dashes (use commas, colons, parentheses or two sentences).
   - CPT/OPT context (from the vault, `10_Areas/Career/VISAs & Work Auth/`):
     - UMN ISSS paused CPT after the August 2026 SEVP broadcast messages (BCM 2608-01 and 2608-02). The new reading: CPT only if training is required of all students in the curriculum.
     - Nov 6 is a **federal court hearing** (AAU v. DHS), not a UMN decision. The government's response was due Oct 28.
     - Live routes per `Junior Year Internship Decision`:
       - on-campus research (core lane);
       - pre-completion OPT (decide around March 2027; ~5 months USCIS wait; $2,250 with premium processing; counts 50% for part-time);
       - an internship physically abroad;
       - CPT if it is restored.
     - ISSS warned against describing projects or volunteer work as jobs.
     - Gates: Oct 30 (ISSS answers), Nov 6 (hearing), Dec 2026, Feb 2027, early Mar 2027.
   - Mentoring Agreement: 3 SMART goals, due to Katie (ksobo@umn.edu) by **2026-11-02**, which is before the Nov 6 hearing. So Goal 1 must be worded to hold either way.
   - CSE "Meet Your Partner" event: Wed Nov 4, 2026, 7–8:30 pm, McNamara Alumni Center.

## The new kit
Every meeting now makes up to three notes and no others, all run by one workflow:

| Note | Standard | Template |
|---|---|---|
| Transcript | `Transcript Standard` (new) | `Transcript Template` (new) |
| Brief | `Brief Standard` (made generic, not mentorship-specific) | `Brief Template` (unchanged) |
| Action | `Action Standard` (made generic) | `Action Template` (unchanged) |

- **Workflow:** `Meeting Workflow` has nine steps and replaces `Transcript to Brief` and `Brief to Action`. Step 2 decides which notes a meeting earns. Every meeting keeps its transcript. A brief follows if there was a decision or a thread worth keeping. An action note follows only if the follow-ups each need a concrete step. The workflow also says not to add prep, summary or log notes.
- **Deleted from `30_Order`:** `Mentorship Meeting Standard`, `Mentorship Meeting Template`, `Transcript to Brief`, `Brief to Action` and `For Transcript`. Git still has them.
- **Updated:** the `Note Type Registry` (mentorship-meeting row removed, transcript row added), the workflows index, and `Templates/MOC.md`.
- **MOC note:** that note is `status: tree`, so by your own rule I should have proposed the change first. It was a one-line link repair.
- **Transcript placement:** the Transcript Standard covers notes in `20_Progress/` and `10_Areas/`. Course video transcripts and career fair captures are excluded so they don't collide with their own Standards.

## The Jesse transcript
I cut everything from "You: Yo." onward, which is where the club meeting starts. The transcript has no timestamps, so that speaker handoff was my cut point. The 535 raw lines before it are byte-identical to the original. I added the date, participants, a "Captured with" line that records the Zoom to Google Meet switch and the cut, and `next`. The note passes lint. The deleted club portion is only in git history if the last auto-sync committed it.

```
Standards/Conformance Standard.md
23:This standard defines "fit" so the goal of 90% of notes meeting their Standard is a number, not a feeling. Every note in the vault has a tier. The tier decides how much of it is checked. Prose quality is not part of fit; that stays with [[HUMAN_WRITING]] and is sampled in review.
27:| 1 | Authored knowledge and work: concept, class, project, plan, evergreen, tracker, reference, index, action, output, review | All six |
28:| 2 | Records: logs, conversation exports, session notes, daily and weekly notes | Checks 1, 3, 4, 5, 6 (headings are not enforced) |
29:| 3 | `type: generated` notes written by a tool | Checks 1, 4, 6 with the generated key set |
30:| 4 | Tool-native files: skills, agents, commands, vendor mirrors | None. Listed by path in [[Note Type Registry]] |
32:## The six checks
33:1. **Frontmatter.** Required keys present, `type`, `status`, and each `*_kind` in the vocabulary of [[Frontmatter Standard]], YAML types correct, no duplicate keys, no byte-order mark, no unresolved placeholder in a value.
34:2. **Headings.** The spec's `##` headings appear in order, and none is empty or holds only a template comment.
35:3. **Links.** Every wikilink in frontmatter resolves. A body link resolves or is labeled `(to create)`.
36:4. **Placement.** The path matches the Standard's `match.path`.
37:5. **Hygiene.** No `---` in the body, the blank-line rule of [[Jarvis Writing and Formatting]] holds, no trailing blank lines, and no leftover `<% %>`, `{{ }}`, `YYYY-MM-DD`, or `== ==`.
38:6. **Mapped.** The note matches exactly one family spec. A note that matches none is `unmapped`, which means the family has no Standard yet. A note that matches several is `ambiguous`, which means two specs overlap.
39:A check passes or fails. There is no partial credit toward the target. A note that fails exactly one check is `near-fit` and is listed first for fixing.
41:`fit % = notes that pass every check for their tier / notes in scope`. [[Vault Lint]] reports it overall, per tier, and per family. The target is 90% overall, with every family above 50%. A family stuck under 50% after four weekly reviews gets a scripted fix or a redesign of its Standard, because a Standard nobody can meet is the Standard's fault.
```

````
# Transcript Standard
==A transcript is the conversation exactly as the tool heard it. Nothing in it is corrected, so every brief and action built from it can be checked against it.==
This is the standard for the raw record of a meeting, call, or other conversation between people, kept in `20_Progress/` or `10_Areas/`. One conversation is one note. [[Meeting Workflow]] makes the brief and the action note from it. Course video transcripts and career fair captures keep their own folders and Standards, and video transcripts in `60_Claude/05_Clippings/` follow the [[Clipping Standard]].
## Spec
```json
{
  "family": "transcript",
  "tier": 2,
  "match": {"type": "input", "kind": "transcript", "path": "^(20_Progress/(?!Degree/|Internship/Career Fair)|10_Areas/)", "where": {}, "where_not": {}},
  "status": ["seed", "sprout", "tree", "archived"],
  "required_keys": ["type", "status", "created", "updated", "tags", "input_kind", "related_progress", "next"],
  "key_types": {"created": "date", "updated": "date", "tags": "list", "related_progress": "list", "next": "text?"},
  "headings": ["Raw Transcript"],
  "optional_headings": []
}
```
## Headings
### Frontmatter
`type: input`, `input_kind: transcript`, `status: seed`. `related_progress` lists the projects the conversation touched. `next` names the brief once it exists, or says why none is planned. Status becomes `sprout` when the brief links back.
> [!WARNING]
> A blank `created`. Every transcript copied from the old starter had one, so none could be sorted by date.
### Title block
Three bold lines under the title: `**Date:**` (the meeting date and time), `**Participants:**` (names and roles), `**Captured with:**` (the tool, the platform, and any cut). Metadata only, no summary.
*Density:* one line each.
### Raw Transcript
The capture inside a single fence, with the speaker labels the tool wrote, no blank lines between turns. Never clean, correct, reorder, or summarize it. The one allowed cut is audio from a different conversation that the same recording caught; the Captured with line names the cut.
> [!WARNING]
> Fixing a garbled word. A guess becomes a fact in the brief. The brief names the passage in Open Questions instead.
### Name and place
The file name carries the meeting and its date and is unique in the vault, because wikilinks resolve by name. It sits in a `Transcripts/` folder next to the project's other meeting notes.
## Legacy Shapes
| Old shape | New shape | Move |
|---|---|---|
| `created:` blank | The capture date | Mechanical |
| `**Captured:**` and `**Source:**` lines | `**Date:**`, `**Participants:**`, `**Captured with:**` | Mechanical |
| The "Paste the untouched transcript" instruction left in the body | Delete it | Mechanical |
| Transcript text outside a fence, or blank lines between turns | Wrap in one fence, drop the blank lines | Mechanical |
| Two conversations in one recording | Cut the second, or split it into its own note | Judgment, needs a yes |
| A transcript in a course folder | Not this family; leave it with the course | Judgment |
## Done Conditions
- The raw text matches what the tool captured, apart from a named cut.
- Date, participants, and capture tool are filled in.
- `next` names the brief, or says none is planned.
- The note passes [[Vault Lint]] for the `transcript` family.
## Gold Standard Example
[[20_Progress/Mentorship Program/Meetings/Transcripts/Jesse Meeting - 28th Oct|Jesse Meeting - 28th Oct]]: a call that dropped from Zoom to Google Meet, fenced and unedited, with the club meeting that followed cut and named on the Captured with line.

````

```
---
```

````
notes:
  - "[[Transcript Standard]]"
  - "[[Action Standard]]"
  - "[[Spec and Plan Standard]]"
  - "[[HUMAN_WRITING]]"
---
# Brief Standard
==A brief compresses one transcript into what was decided and what actually mattered. It is not a second transcript and it is not a task list.==
This is the standard for the note [[Meeting Workflow]] makes from a [[Transcript Standard]] note. A meeting gets a brief when it carried a decision or a thread worth keeping; otherwise its transcript stands alone. A project plan or spec is not a brief even when it sits in `40_Project_Briefs/`; those are [[Spec and Plan Standard]] notes. A summary of an AI conversation is a [[Conversations]] note.
## Spec
```json
{
  "family": "brief",
  "tier": 1,
  "match": {"type": "input", "kind": "brief", "path": "^(20_Progress|10_Areas|60_Claude/40_Project_Briefs)/", "where": {}, "where_not": {}},
  "legacy_match": [{"type": "input", "path": "^(20_Progress|10_Areas)/.*/Briefs/", "where": {"input_kind": "transcript"}}],
  "status": ["seed", "sprout", "tree", "archived"],
  "required_keys": ["type", "status", "created", "updated", "tags", "input_kind", "source_note", "next"],
  "key_types": {"created": "date", "updated": "date", "tags": "list", "source_note": "wikilink"},
  "headings": ["What This Was", "What Was Decided", "Key Threads", "Open Questions", "Follow-Up Actions", "Related Notes"],
  "optional_headings": []
}
```
## Headings
### Frontmatter
`type: input`, `input_kind: brief`, `status: sprout`. `source_note` is a quoted wikilink to the transcript, filename only, no folder path. `related_progress` lists the projects it touches. `next` is the action note as a wikilink once one exists; before that it is the `/note-to-actions` pass, or `None` with the reason.
> [!WARNING]
> `source_note` pointing at a transcript that does not exist, or at a folder path.
### Source lines
`**Source:**` (the meeting in a few words: who, what, where), `**Transcript:**` (a wikilink), `**Date of conversation:**`, directly under the title. Metadata only. If the filename date differs from the meeting date, say so on the date line.
### What This Was
One to three sentences: what kind of conversation, who was in it, and any known limit such as unreliable speaker labels.
### What Was Decided
One bullet per real decision, with a bold label. If nothing was decided, write that; do not invent a resolution for a thread the transcript left open.
### Key Threads
One `###` per substantive topic, ordered by what mattered most. Bold key names on first mention and link the vault notes each thread touches.
*Density:* the bulk of the note. Skip ten-second tangents.
> [!WARNING]
> A thread for every topic touched. That is a second transcript.
### Open Questions
`- [ ]` tasks: unresolved threads, passages too garbled to write up, and context the transcript assumed that the vault lacks.
### Follow-Up Actions
`- [ ]` high-level next steps only. Link the [[Action Standard]] note that breaks them down; when none is needed, one line says so.
### Related Notes
Verified wikilinks only, including the transcript and the action note when one exists.
## Legacy Shapes
| Old shape | New shape | Move |
|---|---|---|
| `input_kind: transcript` on the brief | `input_kind: brief`; the raw transcript keeps `transcript` | Mechanical |
| `source_note: Mentor Meeting Transcript.md` as plain text | `source_note: "[[Mentor Meeting Transcript]]"` | Mechanical |
| A project plan, code doc, or prompt in `40_Project_Briefs/` | Not a brief: retype by [[Spec and Plan Standard]], [[Code Mirror Doc Standard]], or [[Agent Prompt Standard]] | Judgment |
| Brief with no Open Questions or Follow-Up Actions section | Add them, empty only if truly none, and say so | Judgment |
## Done Conditions
- The raw transcript is untouched and `source_note` resolves to it.
- Reopening the transcript would not reveal a decision or thread this brief missed.
- Every garbled or ambiguous passage is named in Open Questions, not guessed.
- Follow-Up Actions links the action note, or says none is needed.
- The note passes [[Vault Lint]] for the `brief` family.
## Gold Standard Example
[[20_Progress/Mentorship Program/Meetings/Briefs/Project Briefings - 2026-07-13|Project Briefings - 2026-07-13]]: a framing line about unreliable speaker labels, decisions with bold labels, ordered threads, and honest open questions. It is the only brief in the vault.

````

````
notes:

  - "[[Transcript Standard]]"
  - "[[HUMAN_WRITING]]"
---
# Action Standard
==An action note is a linked map of what still needs doing: statements only, and every statement earns its place by carrying a link.==
This is the standard for a note derived from a brief or any dense note with interlinks. [[Meeting Workflow]] writes one when a brief's follow-ups each need a concrete step; a meeting that needs none gets no action note. It is a map of next steps, not a second brief and not a checklist. It sits next to its source, in an `Action/` folder if one exists.
## Spec
```json
{
  "family": "action",
  "tier": 1,
  "match": {"type": "action", "kind": null, "path": "", "where": {}, "where_not": {}},
  "legacy_match": [{"type": "project", "path": "^(20_Progress|10_Areas)/.*/Action/"}],
  "status": ["active", "paused", "complete", "archived"],
  "required_keys": ["type", "status", "created", "updated", "tags", "source_note", "next"],
  "key_types": {"created": "date", "updated": "date", "tags": "list", "source_note": "wikilink"},
  "headings": ["Open Threads"],
  "optional_headings": []
}
```
## Headings
### Frontmatter
`type: action`, `status: active`. `source_note` is a quoted wikilink to the brief or note it came from. `related_progress` lists the projects it touches. `next` is the single next statement to close out.
> [!WARNING]
> `type: project` used for an action note. The 2026-07-14 Mentor Meeting actions note was typed `project`, so it did not appear as an action.
### Opening line
One line naming the source and the rule: statements only, every statement links to what it is about.
### Thread sections
One `##` per thread, matching the source's Key Threads in the same order. These headings vary by source, so the Spec does not list them. Under each, short declarative statements, each with at least one wikilink to the project, concept, person, or date it concerns.
*Density:* one sentence per statement. A step that needs a paragraph is explained in the source, not here.
> [!WARNING]
> "Follow up on the resume review" with no link. A statement with no link was not mapped back to anything.
### Open Threads
Last. Statements naming what could not be turned into a concrete step, so nothing from the source is silently dropped.
## Legacy Shapes
| Old shape | New shape | Move |
|---|---|---|
| `type: project` on a note that is a map of next steps | `type: action` | Mechanical |
| Numbered thread headings, `A Note On What This File Is` | Plain thread headings in source order; move the note-on-this-file text to the opening line | Judgment |
| No `## Open Threads` | Add it, listing what is unresolved, or one statement saying nothing is | Judgment |
| Process rules and dated side sections inside the map (Gaps) | Keep as threads if they carry links; otherwise move to the source | Judgment |
| `source_note` as plain text | Quoted wikilink | Mechanical |
## Done Conditions
- Nearly every statement has a wikilink; no checkboxes, no prose paragraphs.
- Every thread in the source is a section or a named open thread.
- Missing link targets were raised as questions, not created as stub notes.
- The brief's Follow-Up Actions links to this note.
- The note passes [[Vault Lint]] for the `action` family.
## Gold Standard Example
[[10_Areas/AI/Setup/Gaps|Gaps]]: type `action`, statements grouped by thread, a source note, and an Open Threads section. It also carries two dated process rules, which Legacy Shapes treats as judgment.

````

```
# Meeting Workflow
Turn any meeting into up to three linked notes: a transcript, a brief, and an action note. The notes are the same whatever the meeting was about. `/transcript-to-brief` runs steps 3 to 5 and `/note-to-actions` runs steps 6 to 8.
## Use when
A meeting, call, or conversation has been recorded or transcribed, whether or not you took part in it.
## Steps
1. Copy [[Transcript Template]] into a `Transcripts/` folder beside the project's other meeting notes and paste the raw text into the fence, per [[Transcript Standard]]. One conversation per note; cut audio from any other conversation and name the cut.
2. Decide what the meeting needs. Every meeting keeps its transcript. It gets a brief if it carried a decision or a thread worth keeping, and an action note only if the brief's follow-ups each need a concrete step. If it needs neither, set the transcript's `next` to say so and stop.
3. Read [[Brief Standard]], then read the whole transcript once before asking anything.
4. Ask only what the transcript requires: garbled or unattributed passages that change meaning, which threads mattered, and context the vault lacks. Search the vault before linking any name.
5. Copy [[Brief Template]] and write the six sections. Set `source_note` to a quoted wikilink to the transcript, filename only. Save in a sibling `Briefs/` folder if one exists, otherwise beside the transcript.
6. Read [[Action Standard]], then the brief and the notes it links. For each thread ask what the concrete next step is; do not infer one the brief does not support.
7. Copy [[Action Template]]: one `##` per brief thread in order, one linked statement per step, unresolved items under Open Threads. Raise a missing link target as a question, never a stub. Save in a sibling `Action/` folder if one exists, otherwise beside the brief.
8. Link forward: the transcript's `next` names the brief and its status becomes `sprout`; the brief's `next`, Follow-Up Actions, and Related Notes name the action note.
9. Run [[Vault Lint]] on each new note and append one line to the session log.
## Do not
Do not edit the transcript beyond the named cut, guess a garbled passage, or invent a decision for a thread left open. Do not add a prep, summary, or log note for a meeting: planning belongs in the project's own plan note, and a meeting makes these three notes and no others.
## Done when
- The transcript is untouched and the brief and action note each link back to their source.
- Every ambiguous passage is resolved by asking or named in Open Questions.
- Lint reports each note fits its family.

```

```
# Meeting Workflow
Turn any meeting into up to three linked notes: transcript, brief, action. `/transcript-to-brief` runs steps 3 to 5 and `/note-to-actions` runs steps 6 to 8.
## Use when
A meeting, call, or conversation has been recorded or transcribed, whether or not you took part.
## Steps
1. Copy [[Transcript Template]] into a `Transcripts/` folder beside the project's meeting notes and paste the raw text into the fence. One conversation per note; cut audio from any other conversation and name the cut.
2. Decide what the meeting needs. Every meeting keeps its transcript. It gets a brief if it carried a decision or a thread worth keeping, and an action note only if the follow-ups each need a concrete step. If neither, set the transcript's `next` to say so and stop.
3. Read [[Brief Standard]], then the whole transcript once before asking anything.
4. Ask only what the transcript requires: garbled passages that change meaning, which threads mattered, context the vault lacks. Search before linking any name.
5. Copy [[Brief Template]], write the six sections, and set `source_note` to a quoted wikilink to the transcript. Save in a sibling `Briefs/` folder if one exists, otherwise beside the transcript.
6. Read [[Action Standard]], then the brief and the notes it links. For each thread ask for the concrete next step; infer none.
7. Copy [[Action Template]]: one `##` per brief thread in order, one linked statement per step, the unresolved under Open Threads. Ask about a missing link target; never make a stub. Save in a sibling `Action/` folder if one exists, otherwise beside the brief.
8. Link forward: the transcript's `next` names the brief and its status becomes `sprout`; the brief's `next` and Follow-Up Actions name the action note.
9. Run [[Vault Lint]] on each new note and append one line to the session log.
## Do not
Do not edit the transcript beyond the named cut, guess a garbled passage, or invent a decision. Do not add a prep, summary, or log note: planning belongs in the project's own plan note, and a meeting makes these three notes and no others.
## Done when
- The transcript is untouched and the brief and action note link back to their source.
- Every ambiguous passage is resolved or named in Open Questions.
- Lint reports each note fits its family.
```

## Three things from the call that matter
- **The Mentoring Agreement was never raised.** It's due to Katie on Nov 2, two days before the event.
- **Jesse now has a partly wrong picture of the work rule.** You told him it was finalized on Sept 15, that Nov 6 is an "official announcement", and that you can't work on campus. The vault says Nov 6 is a federal court hearing and on-campus work is still allowed. The first action is a short correction to him.
- **The best lead was Jesse's company's Bangalore office.** SymphonyAI has an office in Whitefield, your hometown, which fits the internship-abroad route. The introduction ask you planned was never made.

## The skills
The old files were flat notes, not `SKILL.md`. The Anthropic guide requires that exact file name, which is why Claude Code was loading them through the command wrappers. Changes:
- **Structure:** each skill is now `SKILL.md` plus `references/`. The reference examples are built from the Jesse notes and from Ahnaf's action note, which shows the paragraph-to-statement conversion.
- **Frontmatter:** names are kebab-case and match the folders. Descriptions give what the skill does, when to use it, and when not to, at 562 and 520 characters. Pre-approved tools are read-only plus the lint command, so writes still prompt you.
- **Flow:** each skill is a conversation. Skill 1 reads the whole transcript and proposes a plan. It waits for your answers, drafts, then loops on your changes. Skill 2 asks what each thread needs, by when and with whom, then drafts and loops on your changes.
- **Rule fixes:** the old skills told you to set `input_kind: transcript` on briefs and to write `source_note` as plain text. Both are fixed.
- **Removed:** the two command wrappers (a skill wins over a command of the same name) and the old flat files. The Codex copies in `.agents/skills` are synced.

## The agent
- `meeting-operator` preloads both skills.
- It has six checkpoints where it stops for your input: triage, the brief plan, brief revisions, per-thread action questions, action revisions, and the final report.
- I deleted the old `note-to-actions` agent, since this one replaces it.
- **How to run it:** use `claude --agent meeting-operator`. A subagent can't ask you questions directly, so the agent returns its questions at the end of each phase when run that way. This comes from the Claude Code docs as summarized by a fetch tool, so check it against your build.
- I added the agent to `Orchestration.md`, the `CLAUDE.md` tool table and `Claude Code.md`. The Meeting Workflow names it.

## Meet Your Partner
It's a draft one-pager for Nov 4 with fixed headings, so it can be reused for each meeting. It adds a "Before the Event" checklist, a 30-second intro, the work-authorization pitch, three questions and the introduction ask. The project facts in the intro are July-dated and marked [confirm]. This overwrote the April Ahnaf content, which is in git.

## Overall Plan
I left it empty on purpose, per your sequencing: it covers both mentors and comes after Ahnaf's meetings are mapped. Ahnaf's brief and action note are the next base layer to rebuild, since most of the action note is still paragraphs. I linked the Overall Plan as an open thread in Jesse's action note.

## Test it
- The guide suggests trigger tests. Try `/transcript-to-brief` on the Jesse transcript. It should find the existing brief and ask whether to update it.
- Also try a paraphrase like "brief this call", and one unrelated prompt that should not trigger it.

## Housekeeping
- I started the local MinerU server to read the guide PDF, in its text-only mode, and stopped it afterward.
- I wrote no session-log entry.

```
## 3 Goals
1. Which project is the stronger portfolio artifact and how should I frame it?
2. What should I focus on this summer if there is no internship?
3. How do I network intentionally — not just ask for referrals?
## 2 Project Pitches
**[[Opspilot]]:** AI operations dashboard for a restaurant manager. Connected workflow: reservation → invoice → payment → finance → feedback → recovery action. AI became useful only after the deterministic workflow was solid.

**[[Resq]]:** CFO-style cash workspace. Cash truth, 13-week forecast, breakpoint detection, ranked actions. AI advises but never touches the numbers. Sharper product story after the pivot from OpsPilot.

**Cross-project lesson:** The hardest part was not coding. It was scope control, demo truth, and explaining clearly what was real versus mocked.
## 3 Questions
1. Between OpsPilot and Resq, which sounds more impressive to an engineering manager, and why?
2. If no internship, which 1–2 summer priorities would you weight heaviest: system design, AI/ML, research, project-building, or networking?
3. If I want to learn about product-facing engineering teams like BestBuy.com or mobile, which roles should I talk to first?
## 1 Concrete Ask
"If it feels appropriate, would you be open to introducing me to one frontend engineer and one backend engineer for informational conversations later on?"
## 1 Next Step
Decide whether [[Resq]] or [[Opspilot]] becomes the main summer portfolio project, update the [[Plan - Ahnaf]].
```

```
## Introduction

A [skill](https://claude.com/blog/skills) is a set of instructions - packaged as a simple folder - that teaches Claude how to handle specific tasks or workflows. Skills are one of the most powerful ways to customize Claude for your specific needs. Instead of re-explaining your preferences, processes, and domain expertise in every conversation, skills let you teach Claude once and benefit every time.

Skills are powerful when you have repeatable workflows: generating frontend designs from specs, conducting research with consistent methodology, creating documents that follow your team's style guide, or orchestrating multi-step processes. They work well with Claude's built-in capabilities like code execution and document creation. For those building MCP integrations, skills add another powerful layer helping turn raw tool access into reliable, optimized workflows.

This guide covers everything you need to know to build effective skills - from planning and structure to testing and distribution. Whether you're building a skill for yourself, your team, or for the community, you'll find practical patterns and real-wo
```

```
## Chapter 1
## Fundamentals
## What is a skill?
A skill is a folder containing:
• SKILL.md (required): Instructions in Markdown with YAML frontmatter • scripts/ (optional): Executable code (Python, Bash, etc.) • references/ (optional): Documentation loaded as needed • assets/ (optional): Templates, fonts, icons used in output
## Core design principles
## Progressive Disclosure
Skills use a three-level system:
• First level (YAML frontmatter): Always loaded in Claude's system prompt.
Provides just enough information for Claude to know when each skill should be used without loading all of it into context.
• Second level (SKILL.md body): Loaded when Claude thinks the skill is relevant to the current task. Contains the full instructions and guidance.
• Third level (Linked files): Additional files bundled within the skill directory that Claude can choose to navigate and discover only as needed.
This progressive disclosure minimizes token usage while maintaining specialized expertise.
## Composability
Claude can load multiple skills simultaneously. Your skill should work well alongside others, not assume it's the only capability available.
## Portability
Skills work identically across Claude.ai, Claude Code, and API. Create a skill once and it works across all surfaces without modification, provided the environment supports any dependencies the skill requires.
## For MCP Builders: Skills + Connectors
💡 Building standalone skills without MCP? Skip to Planning and Design - you can always return here later.
If you already have a [working MCP server](https://support.claude.com/en/articles/[REDACTED]), you've done the hard part. Skills are the knowledge layer on top - capturing the workflows and best practices you already know, so Claude can apply them consistently.
## The kitchen analogy
MCP provides the professional kitchen: access to tools, ingredients, and equipment.
Skills provide the recipes: step-by-step instructions on how to create something valuable.
5
<!-- page 6 of 33 -->
Together, they enable users to accomplish complex tasks without needing to figure out every step themselves.
How they work together:
![Image block](doc:66cbcc0/tier:flash/page:6/block:3)
<details>
<summary>image content</summary>
MCP (Connectivity)        Skills (Knowledge)
Connects Claude to your service
Teaches Claude how to use your service
(Notion, Asana, Linear, etc.)
effectively
Provides real-time data access and tool
Captures workflows and best practices
invocation
What Claude can do        How Claude should do it
</details>
## Why this matters for your MCP users
## Without skills:
• Users connect your MCP but don't know what to do next
• Support tickets asking "how do I do X with your integration" • Each conversation starts from scratch
• Inconsistent results because users prompt differently each time
• Users blame your connector when the real issue is workflow guidance
## With skills:
• Pre-built workflows activate automatically when needed • Consistent, reliable tool usage • Best practices embedded in every interaction • Lower learning curve for your integration

Planning and design

## Chapter 2
## Planning and design
## Start with use cases
## Common skill use case categories
Before writing any code, identify 2-3 concrete use cases your skill should enable.
## Good use case definition:
At Anthropic, we’ve observed three common use cases:
## Category 1: Document & Asset Creation
Used for: Creating consistent, high-quality output including documents, presentations, apps, designs, code, etc.
Use Case: Project Sprint Planning
Trigger: User says "help me plan this sprint" or "create sprint tasks" Steps:
Real example: [frontend-design skill](https://github.com/anthropics/skills/tree/main/skills/frontend-design) (also see [skills for docx, pptx, xlsx, and ppt](https://github.com/anthropics/skills/tree/main/skills))
"Create distinctive, production-grade frontend interfaces with high design quality. Use when building web components, pages, artifacts, posters, or applications."
1. Fetch current project status from Linear (via MCP)
2. Analyze team velocity and capacity
3. Suggest task prioritization
4. Create tasks in Linear with proper labels and estimates Result: Fully planned sprint with tasks created
## Ask yourself:
• What does a user want to accomplish?
• What multi-step workflows does this require?
• Which tools are needed (built-in or MCP?)
## Key techniques:
• Embedded style guides and brand standards • Template structures for consistent output • Quality checklists before finalizing
• No external tools required - uses Claude's built-in capabilities
• What domain knowledge or best practices should be embedded?
8
<!-- page 9 of 33 -->
## Category 2: Workflow Automation
## Define success criteria
Used for: Multi-step processes that benefit from consistent methodology, including coordination across multiple MCP servers.
## How will you know your skill is working?
These are aspirational targets - rough benchmarks rather than precise thresholds. Aim for rigor but accept that there will be an element of vibes-based assessment. We are actively developing more robust measurement guidance and tooling.
Real example: [skill-creator skill](https://github.com/anthropics/skills/blob/main/skills/skill-creator/SKILL.md)
"Interactive guide for creating new skills. Walks the user through use case definition, frontmatter generation, instruction writing, and validation."
## Key techniques:
## Quantitative metrics:
• Skill triggers on 90% of relevant queries
– How to measure: Run 10-20 test queries that should trigger your skill. Track how many times it loads automatically vs. requires explicit invocation. • Completes workflow in X tool calls
– How to measure: Compare the same task with and without the skill enabled.
• Step-by-step workflow with validation gates • Templates for common structures • Built-in review and improvement suggestions • Iterative refinement loops
## Category 3: MCP Enhancement
Count tool calls and total tokens consumed.
• 0 failed API calls per workflow
– How to measure: Monitor MCP server logs during test runs. Track retry rates and error codes.
Used for: Workflow guidance to enhance the tool access an MCP server provides. Real example: [sentry-code-review skill (from Sentry)](https://github.com/getsentry/sentry-for-claude/tree/main/skills) "Automatically analyzes and fixes detected bugs in GitHub Pull Requests using Sentry's error monitoring data via their MCP server."
## Qualitative metrics:
• Users don't need to prompt Claude about next steps – How to assess: During testing, note how often you need to redirect or clarify.
## Key techniques:
Ask beta users for feedback.
• Workflows complete without user correction
– How to assess: Run the same request 3-5 times. Compare outputs for structural consistency and quality.
• Consistent results across sessions
– How to assess: Can a new user accomplish the task on first try with minimal guidance?
• Coordinates multiple MCP calls in sequence • Embeds domain expertise • Provides context users would otherwise need to specify • Error handling for common MCP issues
9
<!-- page 10 of 33 -->
## YAML frontmatter: The most important part
## Technical requirements
The YAML frontmatter is how Claude decides whether to load your skill. Get this right.
## File structure
## your-skill-name/
├── SKILL.md
\# Required - main skill file
## Minimal required format
├── scripts/
\# Optional - executable code
## ---
name: your-skill-name description: What it does. Use when user asks to [specific phrases]. ---
│ ├── process\_data.py # Example
│ └── validate.sh # Example
├── references/
\# Optional - documentation
│ ├── api-guide.md # Example
│ └── examples/ # Example
└── assets/
\# Optional - templates, etc.
└── report-template.md # Example
## Critical rules
## SKILL.md naming:
That's all you need to start.
## Field requirements
name (required):
• Must be exactly SKILL.md (case-sensitive) • No variations accepted (SKILL.MD, skill.md, etc.)
## Skill folder naming:
• kebab-case only
## • Use kebab-case: notion-project-setup ✅
## • No spaces: Notion Project Setup ❌
• No spaces or capitals • Should match folder name description (required): • MUST include BOTH: – What the skill does
## • No underscores: notion\_project\_setup ❌
## • No capitals: NotionProjectSetup ❌
## No README.md:
– When to use it (trigger conditions)
• Under 1024 characters
• No XML tags (< or >)
• Include specific tasks users might say
• Don't include README.md inside your skill folder • All documentation goes in SKILL.md or references/
• Mention file types if relevant
• Note: when distributing via GitHub, you'll still want a repo-level README for human users — see Distribution and Sharing.

## The description field
According to Anthropic's [engineering blog](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/equipping-agents-for-the-real-world-with-agent-skills): "This metadata...provides just enough information for Claude to know when each skill should be used without loading all of it into context." This is the first level of progressive disclosure.
compatibility (optional) • 1-500 characters

metadata (optional): • Any custom key-value pairs • Suggested: author, version, mcp-server
```

```
## An open standard
We've published [Agent Skills](https://agentskills.io/home) as an open standard. Like MCP, we believe skills should be portable across tools and platforms - the same skill should work whether you're using Claude or other AI platforms. That said, some skills are designed to take full advantage of a specific platform's capabilities; authors can note this in the skill's compatibility field. We've been collaborating with members of the ecosystem on the standard, and we're excited by early adoption.

## Positioning your skill
Start by hosting your skill on GitHub with a public repo, clear README (for human visitors — this is separate from your skill folder, which should not contain a README.md), and example usage with screenshots. Then add a section to your MCP documentation that links to the skill, explains why using both together is valuable, and provides a quick-start guide.
How you describe your skill determines whether users understand its value and actually try it. When writing about your skill—in your README, documentation, or marketing - keep these principles in mind.

## Focus on outcomes, not features:

## Chapter 5
## Patterns and troubleshooting
## Pattern 1: Sequential workflow orchestration
These patterns emerged from skills created by early adopters and internal teams. They represent common approaches we've seen work well, not prescriptive templates.
Use when: Your users need multi-step processes in a specific order. Example structure:
## Choosing your approach: Problem-first vs. tool-first
Think of it like Home Depot. You might walk in with a problem - "I need to fix a kitchen cabinet" - and an employee points you to the right tools. Or you might pick out a new drill and ask how to use it for your specific job.
Skills work the same way:
## # Workflow: Onboard New Customer
## ### Step 1: Create Account
Call MCP tool: \`create\_customer\` Parameters: name, email, company
• Problem-first: "I need to set up a project workspace" → Your skill orchestrates the right MCP calls in the right sequence. Users describe outcomes; the skill handles the tools.
## ### Step 2: Setup Payment
Call MCP tool: \`setup\_payment\_method\` Wait for: payment method verification
• Tool-first: "I have Notion MCP connected" → Your skill teaches Claude the optimal workflows and best practices. Users have access; the skill provides expertise.
## ### Step 3: Create Subscription
Call MCP tool: \`create\_subscription\` Parameters: plan\_id, customer\_id (from Step 1)
Most skills lean one direction. Knowing which framing fits your use case helps you choose the right pattern below.
## ### Step 4: Send Welcome Email
Call MCP tool: \`send\_email\` Template: welcome\_email\_template
Key techniques:
• Explicit step ordering • Dependencies between steps • Validation at each stage • Rollback instructions for failures
22
<!-- page 23 of 33 -->
## Pattern 2: Multi-MCP coordination
## Pattern 3: Iterative refinement
Use when: Workflows span multiple services.
Use when: Output quality improves with iteration. Example: Report generation
Example: Design-to-development handoff
\### Phase 1: Design Export (Figma MCP)
1. Export design assets from Figma
2. Generate design specifications
3. Create asset manifest
## # Iterative Report Creation
## ### Initial Draft
1. Fetch data via MCP
2. Generate first draft report
\### Phase 2: Asset Storage (Drive MCP)
1. Create project folder in Drive
2. Upload all assets
3. Save to temporary file
## ### Quality Check
1. Run validation script: \`scripts/check\_report.py\`
3. Generate shareable links
## ### Phase 3: Task Creation (Linear MCP)
1. Create development tasks
2. Attach asset links to tasks
3. Assign to engineering team
2. Identify issues: - Missing sections - Inconsistent formatting - Data validation errors
## ### Refinement Loop
## ### Phase 4: Notification (Slack MCP) 1. Post handoff summary to #engineering 2. Include asset links and task references
1. Address each identified issue
2. Regenerate affected sections
3. Re-validate
4. Repeat until quality threshold met
Key techniques:
• Clear phase separation
• Data passing between MCPs
• Validation before moving to next phase • Centralized error handling
## ### Finalization
1. Apply final formatting
2. Generate summary
3. Save final version
Key techniques:
• Explicit quality criteria • Iterative improvement • Validation scripts • Know when to stop iterating

## Pattern 4: Context-aware tool selection
## Pattern 5: Domain-specific intelligence
Use when: Same outcome, different tools depending on context. Example: File storage
Use when: Your skill adds specialized knowledge beyond tool access. Example: Financial compliance
## # Smart File Storage
## ### Decision Tree
1. Check file type and size
## # Payment Processing with Compliance
\### Before Processing (Compliance Check)
1. Fetch transaction details via MCP
2. Determine best storage location: - Large files (>10MB): Use cloud storage MCP - Collaborative docs: Use Notion/Docs MCP - Code files: Use GitHub MCP - Temporary files: Use local storage
## ### Execute Storage
Based on decision: - Call appropriate MCP tool - Apply service-specific metadata - Generate access link
\### Provide Context to User Explain why that storage was chosen
Key techniques:
• Clear decision criteria
• Fallback options
• Transparency about choices
2. Apply compliance rules: - Check sanctions lists - Verify jurisdiction allowances - Assess risk level
3. Document compliance decision ### Processing IF compliance passed: - Call payment processing MCP tool - Apply appropriate fraud checks - Process transaction
ELSE:
\- Flag for review - Create compliance case
\### Audit Trail - Log all compliance checks - Record processing decisions - Generate audit report
Key techniques: • Domain expertise embedded in logic • Compliance before action • Comprehensive documentation • Clear governance

## Troubleshooting
## Skill won't upload
## # Wrong
name: My Cool Skill
Error: "Could not find SKILL.md in uploaded folder" Cause: File not named exactly SKILL.md
## # Correct
name: my-cool-skill
Solution: • Rename to SKILL.md (case-sensitive) • Verify with: ls -la should show SKILL.md Error: "Invalid frontmatter"
## Skill doesn't trigger
Symptom: Skill never loads automatically Fix: Revise your description field. See The Description Field for good/bad examples. Quick checklist: • Is it too generic? ("Helps with projects" won't work) • Does it include trigger phrases users would actually say? • Does it mention relevant file types if applicable? Debugging approach: Ask Claude: "When would you use the [skill name] skill?" Claude will quote the description back. Adjust based on what's missing.
Cause: YAML formatting issue Common mistakes:
## # Wrong - missing delimiters
name: my-skill
description: Does things
## # Wrong - unclosed quotes
name: my-skill
description: "Does things
## # Correct ---
## Skill triggers too often
Symptom: Skill loads for unrelated queries Solutions: 1. Add negative triggers
name: my-skill
description: Does things
\---
Error: "Invalid skill name"
## description: Advanced data analysis for CSV files. Use for statistical modeling, regression, clustering. Do NOT use for
Cause: Name has spaces or capitals
simple data exploration (use data-viz skill instead).

2. Be more specific

3. Clarify scope
1. Instructions too verbose – Keep instructions concise – Use bullet points and numbered lists – Move detailed reference to separate files
## description: PayFlow payment processing for e-commerce. Use
specifically for online payment workflows, not for general financial queries.
## MCP connection issues
Symptom: Skill loads but MCP calls fail
2. Instructions buried – Put critical instructions at the top – Use ## Important or ## Critical headers – Repeat key points if needed
3. Ambiguous language
## # Bad
Make sure to validate things properly
## # Good
CRITICAL: Before calling create\_project, verify: - Project name is non-empty - At least one team member assigned - Start date is not in the past
Checklist:
1. Verify MCP server is connected – Claude.ai: Settings > Extensions > [Your Service] – Should show "Connected" status
Advanced technique: For critical validations, consider bundling a script that performs the checks programmatically rather than relying on language instructions. Code is deterministic; language interpretation isn't. See the [Office skills](https://github.com/anthropics/skills/tree/main/skills) for examples of this pattern.
2. Check authentication – API keys valid and not expired – Proper permissions/scopes granted – OAuth tokens refreshed
3. Test MCP independently – Ask Claude to call MCP directly (without skill) – "Use [Service] MCP to fetch my projects" – If this fails, issue is MCP not skill
4. Model "laziness" Add explicit encouragement:
## # Performance Notes
\- Take your time to do this thoroughly - Quality is more important than speed - Do not skip validation steps
Note: Adding this to user prompts is more effective than in SKILL.md
4. Verify tool names – Skill references correct MCP tool names – Check MCP server documentation – Tool names are case-sensitive
26
<!-- page 27 of 33 -->
## Large context issues
Symptom: Skill seems slow or responses degraded
Causes:
• Skill content too large • Too many skills enabled simultaneously • All content loaded instead of progressive disclosure
Solutions:
1. Optimize SKILL.md size – Move detailed docs to references/ – Link to references instead of inline – Keep SKILL.md under 5,000 words
2. Reduce enabled skills – Evaluate if you have more than 20 - 50 skills enabled simultaneously – Recommend selective enablement – Consider skill "packs" for related capabilities

## Security notes
## Allowed:
• Any standard YAML types (strings, numbers, booleans, lists, objects) • Custom metadata fields • Long descriptions (up to 1024 characters)
frontmatter
```

```
## Chapter 3
## Testing and iteration
Skills can be tested at varying levels of rigor depending on your needs:
• Manual testing in Claude.ai - Run queries directly and observe behavior. Fast iteration, no setup required.
• Scripted testing in Claude Code - Automate test cases for repeatable validation across changes.
• Programmatic testing via skills API - Build evaluation suites that run systematically against defined test sets.
Choose the approach that matches your quality requirements and the visibility of your skill. A skill used internally by a small team has different testing needs than one deployed to thousands of enterprise users.
Pro Tip: Iterate on a single task before expanding
We’ve found that the most effective skill creators iterate on a single challenging task until Claude succeeds, then extract the winning approach into a skill. This leverages Claude’s in-context learning and provides faster signal than broad testing. Once you have a working foundation, expand to multiple test cases for coverage.
## Recommended Testing Approach
Based on early experience, effective skills testing typically covers three areas:
## 1. Triggering tests
Goal: Ensure your skill loads at the right times.
Test cases: • ✅ Triggers on obvious tasks • ✅ Triggers on paraphrased requests • ❌ Doesn't trigger on unrelated topics Example test suite:
Should trigger: - "Help me set up a new ProjectHub workspace" - "I need to create a project in ProjectHub" - "Initialize a ProjectHub project for Q4 planning"
Should NOT trigger: - "What's the weather in San Francisco?" - "Help me write Python code" - "Create a spreadsheet" (unless ProjectHub skill handles sheets)
15
<!-- page 16 of 33 -->
## 2. Functional tests
## With skill:
\- Automatic workflow execution - 2 clarifying questions only - 0 failed API calls - 6,000 tokens consumed
## Using the skill-creator skill
The skill-creator skill - available in Claude.ai via plugin directory or download for Claude Code - can help you build and iterate on skills. If you have an MCP server and know your top 2–3 workflows, you can build and test a functional skill in a single sitting - often in 15–30 minutes.
Goal: Verify the skill produces correct outputs. Test cases: • Valid outputs generated • API calls succeed • Error handling works • Edge cases covered Example:
## Test: Create project with 5 tasks
Given: Project name "Q4 Planning", 5 task descriptions When: Skill executes workflow Then:
\- Project created in ProjectHub
\- 5 tasks created with correct properties - All tasks linked to project
\- No API errors
Creating skills: • Generate skills from natural language descriptions • Produce properly formatted SKILL.md with frontmatter • Suggest trigger phrases and structure Reviewing skills:
• Flag common issues (vague descriptions, missing triggers, structural problems)
## 3. Performance comparison
Goal: Prove the skill improves results vs. baseline.
• Identify potential over/under-triggering risks
Use the metrics from <u>Define Success Criteria</u>. Here's what a comparison might look like.
• Suggest test cases based on the skill's stated purpose Iterative improvement:
• After using your skill and encountering edge cases or failures, bring those examples back to skill-creator
Baseline comparison:
• Example: "Use the issues & solution identified in this chat to improve how the skill handles [specific edge case]"
## Without skill: - User provides instructions each time - 15 back-and-forth messages
\- 3 failed API calls requiring retry - 12,000 tokens consumed

<!-- page 17 of 33 -->
To use:
Execution issues:
• Inconsistent results
"Use the skill-creator skill to help me build a skill for • API call failures
[your use case]" • User corrections needed
Note: skill-creator helps you design and refine skills but does not execute
automated test suites or produce quantitative evaluation results.
## Iteration based on feedback
Skills are living documents. Plan to iterate based on:
## Undertriggering signals:
• Skill doesn't load when it should • Users manually enabling it • Support questions about when to use it
Solution: Improve instructions, add error handling
Solution: Add more detail and nuance to the description - this may include keywords particularly for technical terms
## Overtriggering signals:
• Skill loads for irrelevant queries • Users disabling it • Confusion about purpose
Solution: Add negative triggers, be more specific
```

```
- **Skill (a `/command`):** a repeatable procedure with a fixed checklist, run in the main window. Examples: `/startday`, `/closeday`, `/ingest-clipping`, `/weekly-review`.
- **Agent:** isolated context for heavy reading or a distinct role.
- **Inline:** everything else.
## Agent file shape
Each agent is `.claude/agents/<name>.md` with frontmatter `name`, `description`, `tools`, and `model`. The description is third person and says when to use it: "Use proactively for... MUST BE USED for...". The `tools` list holds only what the role needs, so a read-only role cannot write.
## Roster
| Agent | Use for | Tools |
|---|---|---|
| `ingestion` | any new source: YouTube, transcript, clipping, PDF | Read, Write, WebFetch, Bash |
| `research-distiller` | deep multi-source or long-PDF distillation | Read, Glob, Grep, Edit, Write, Bash, WebFetch |
| `human-operator` | default writer for new vault prose | Read, Write, Edit |
| `anti-slop-editor` | rewriting AI-sounding notes | Read, Glob, Grep, Edit, Write |
| `note-to-actions` | turning a note into an action map | Read, Write |
| `learning-agent` | drills, spaced repetition, learning from the vault only | Read, Glob, Grep, Edit, Write |
| `professor` | coordinating coursework skills | Read, Grep, Glob, Edit |
| `daily-operator` | dashboard, plans, rollover, deadlines | Read, Grep, Glob, Edit |
| `career-operator` | applications, resume, portfolio briefs | Read, Glob, Grep, Edit, Write |
| `vault-curator` | links, orphans, duplicates, Write Contract checks | Read, Glob, Grep, Edit, Write |
| `llm-council` | high-stakes decisions needing five advisors | Read, Grep, Glob |
Counts and tool lists drift. The folder `.claude/agents/` is the truth; fix this table when it disagrees.
> [!WARNING]
> Treating a subagent's report as fact. A survey agent once returned a "will report back" line with zero tool uses recorded. Check tool-use counts, and read the files that carry the conclusion before acting on it.

| `/remove-ai-slop` | Rewrites AI-sounding prose per [[HUMAN_WRITING]] |
| `/context` | Loads the context pack: manifest, dashboard, session log tail |

| `/weekly-review` | Answers the 7 weekly questions, writes the weekly note |

| `/tag-month` | Creates missing monthly checkpoint git tags |

| `/ideas` | Generates a grounded idea report — tools to build, people to meet, topics to investigate |
| `/llm-council` | Runs a high-stakes, genuinely uncertain decision through a 5-advisor council with peer review and chairman synthesis |
| `/note-to-actions` | Turns a note into a link-dense map of concrete next steps |
| `/strategy` | Runs a project, trade, or resource-allocation decision through SWOT, WARGAME, PARETO, and LEVERAGE |
| `/transcript-to-brief` | Turns a transcript into a structured brief |
Only `startday`, `closeday`, and `ingesting-clipping` follow the directory standard from [[Jarvis OS — North Star]] Part 5.1 (SKILL.md ≤500 lines + reference.md + scripts/). The rest are still flat prose files — convert them as they get used, most-used first.
```
